> 1. There are three axioms where the conventional account implicitly presupposed eight or more assumptions. The reduction is striking. Most of what the growth mindset framework claims is either derivable from these three foundations or is overstated.
> 2. The simplicity of Axiom 1 is not a disappointment — it reflects genuine theoretical compression. The insight that failure attribution to stable, uncontrollable causes produces helplessness is one of the most powerful and well-verified findings in behavioral science.
> 3. If you could add a fourth axiom, you might propose that "Felt challenge promotes deeper processing than felt mastery" — the "desirable difficulties" axiom from cognitive science. This is well-supported, but it is derivable from Axiom 3 (challenge promotes mastery-goal activation) rather than being independently irreducible.

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[stoicism-codebase-pack]])*
> 1. The convergence with the conventional view at the phenomenological level should be reassuring. The conventional view is approximately right about what good learning environments look like. It is wrong about the mechanism and the intervention strategy.
> 2. The most unsettling divergence may be the implication that the billions of dollars and millions of instructional hours invested in mindset interventions have been aimed at the wrong lever. The cognitive content of the intervention — "your brain is malleable" — is not what helps students. What helps is changing the attribution and goal-orientation dynamics in specific contexts.
> 3. The most important practical change this reconstruction suggests: stop delivering growth mindset as a one-time workshop and start building mastery-experience architectures in specific performance domains.

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[stoicism-codebase-pack]])*
> 1. The revised understanding may leave you somewhat less certain about your ability to easily improve student outcomes through psychological intervention. This is appropriate epistemic calibration, not pessimism.
> 2. The most important practical change: shift from asking "how do I get students to believe intelligence is malleable?" to asking "how do I design this learning sequence so that the feedback environment attributes failures to specific, correctable causes and activates mastery-goal orientation?"
> 3. There is real tension between the revised understanding and much institutional practice — schools have invested substantially in growth mindset programs, teachers have been trained in the language, and dismantling this investment creates friction. The tension is real but should not prevent the better-grounded approach.

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[stoicism-codebase-pack]])*
> 1. This analysis may have clarified the process of first-principles thinking as much as the topic of growth mindset. The key discipline — holding the question "is this an axiom or an assumption?" against every proposition — is applicable to any framework that has achieved cultural authority.
> 2. The most likely place where this analysis carries the analyst's unexamined assumptions is in its privileging of experimental and meta-analytic evidence over qualitative and practitioner evidence. A sophisticated critic would ask: what are the assumptions embedded in the evidence hierarchy itself?
> 3. The topic in your own work or life most likely to benefit from this kind of deconstructive analysis is any domain where you have absorbed a framework so thoroughly that its assumptions feel like facts.

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[stoicism-codebase-pack]])*
> Before we begin dismantling assumptions, take a moment to consider: what do you currently believe about mental models? Do you think human reasoning is fundamentally model-based, rule-based, or something else? Did you arrive at this view by examining evidence, or by absorbing it from coursework, popular accounts (Norman's *The Design of Everyday Things*), or the professional community you inhabit? Note that Johnson-Laird's precise technical sense of "mental model" and the loose colloquial sense are radically different — which one have you been thinking with?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[stoicism-codebase-pack]])*
> Which of these assumption audits produced resistance? If you reacted to the dissolution of the rule/model dichotomy with "but that's obviously one or the other," note that reaction. The dichotomy is one of the field's inherited framings — it shaped careers, textbooks, and funding priorities — and its dissolution is therefore unsettling even when the evidence supports it. Productive discomfort here is the signal that we've found something load-bearing in the conventional understanding.

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[stoicism-codebase-pack]])*
> 1. The most surprising dissolution is probably Assumption 5 — the rule/model dichotomy. If this is an artifact, the *entire framing* of the debate changes, not just the resolution.
> 2. Assumption 6 (content matters) survives but in theoretically neutral form — it no longer serves as evidence *for* models *against* rules.
> 3. The conventional understanding is less stable than it appears: three assumptions dissolved or are suspended, two require significant revision, and only two survive relatively intact.

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[stoicism-codebase-pack]])*
> For Axiom 1: Can you imagine a world in which reasoning errors are random? What would that world's cognitive architecture look like? The fact that it's hard to imagine is evidence of the axiom's robustness — randomness would require that all systematic influences on reasoning (working memory, content, prior knowledge) have *zero net effect*, which is implausible.
> For Axiom 5 (the one we lack): Notice that none of these axioms specify *what kind of representation* is doing the work. That absence is not an oversight — it reflects the genuine state of the evidence.

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[stoicism-codebase-pack]])*
> 1. There are *four* axioms, not Johnson-Laird's rich representational architecture — far fewer than the theory deploys.
> 2. Notably, none of the four axioms require the concept of a "mental model" as technically defined. They require: systematicity, construction, resource-sensitivity, and content-sensitivity. These are compatible with model-based, rule-based, probabilistic, and hybrid architectures.
> 3. The most important missing axiom — the one we cannot establish — is the representational format axiom: whether reasoning proceeds specifically via analogical models. That is not a bedrock truth; it is a theoretical hypothesis about the mechanism that implements the four genuine axioms.

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[stoicism-codebase-pack]])*
> 1. The convergence that is most reassuring: reasoning errors are systematic and arise from the constructive process. This confirms that Johnson-Laird was right to target the *representations* people build as the locus of errors, not the inference rules they apply.
> 2. The divergence that is most unsettling: the specific representational format (analogical mental models) is not established by the evidence that supports the theory's empirical predictions. The question may be unanswerable at the level of behavioral experiments.
> 3. The practical change: interventions aimed at improving reasoning should target the *constructive process* — teaching people to generate alternative representations, to consider what they may have omitted, to interrogate their pragmatic assumptions — rather than specifically "thinking in models" or "applying rules."

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[stoicism-codebase-pack]])*
> 1. This analysis should produce more confidence in the *empirical* core of mental models research (the error patterns, the difficulty effects) and less confidence in the *theoretical* interpretation (that these effects prove analogical representation). One can be a thoroughgoing mental models researcher while agnostic about format.
> 2. The single most important change: the rule/model dichotomy was always a theoretical framing, not a discovery. Dissolving it opens space for pluralistic accounts — perhaps the most important implication for researchers.
> 3. For practitioners: the implication is not "stop teaching mental model building" but "be agnostic about whether the benefit comes from the analogical format or from the process of active construction and alternative generation."

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[stoicism-codebase-pack]])*
> 1. This report has changed the question. We arrived to analyze "what are mental models?" and left asking "what is the right normative standard for human reasoning?" That migration of the fundamental question is itself diagnostic — it suggests that the conventional framing concealed a deeper issue.
> 2. The potential blind spot in this analysis is the analyst's theoretical orientation toward pluralism. A committed mental models theorist would have found different assumptions to dissolve and different axioms to retain. First-principles thinking is not view-from-nowhere thinking.
> 3. The topic in your own thinking most suited to this method: any domain where you hold a strong theoretical preference that has calcified into an assumption. The discomfort of the assumption audit is most productive there.

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. How does COT's positing of three distinct orientations represent a theoretical advance over de Charms' binary origins/pawns distinction? What phenomena can the tripartite model explain that a binary model cannot?
> 2. The claim that COT operates at the "person level" within SDT implies a certain kind of stability. What mechanisms would allow person-level orientations to change? Is COT conceptually committed to orientation stability, or merely to reliability?
> 3. How does the SDT macrotheory's structure — multiple mini-theories addressing distinct explanatory problems — differ from a more unified theoretical approach? What are the scientific advantages and risks of this architectural choice?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. The autonomy orientation seems normatively desirable. If orientations are relatively stable, is COT implicitly a framework that identifies which persons are "well-suited" to autonomous functioning versus those who are not? What are the ethical implications of this framing?
> 2. How does the control orientation differ from conscientiousness or achievement motivation as typically measured in personality research? Is the theoretical distinction clear enough to justify treating them as separable constructs?
> 3. Consider a person who behaves identically across two contexts but one context activates their autonomy orientation and the other activates their control orientation. What would be the differences in their subjective experience, and what evidence could distinguish these cases empirically?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. COT's developmental account implies that orientation change in adulthood is possible but slow. What kind of evidence would be required to demonstrate that an adult has genuinely shifted their dominant causality orientation, as opposed to merely learned to *behave* differently in specific contexts?
> 2. The control and impersonal orientations both involve adverse developmental histories, but the adversity takes different forms (controlling vs. neglectful/chaotic). Do you think these different developmental pathways produce qualitatively different phenomenological experiences in adulthood, even when the behavioral outcomes look similar?
> 3. How should educators think about their students' causality orientation profiles when designing learning environments? What structural features of educational settings might systematically reinforce control or impersonal orientations even in well-intentioned teachers?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. The GCOS measures *self-reported* tendencies in how one would respond to scenarios. How well might self-report capture the genuinely automatic, perceptual components of causality orientations? What measurement strategies beyond self-report might access the attentional dimension of orientations?
> 2. If a researcher wanted to develop a more psychometrically refined version of the GCOS, what design choices would they face regarding breadth versus precision? Would increasing item homogeneity (improving reliability) necessarily reduce construct validity?
> 3. Given that the impersonal orientation subscale shows the lowest reliability, should researchers place less confidence in findings specifically about the impersonal orientation? What would a research agenda focused on improving impersonal orientation measurement look like?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. The affordance mechanism implies that changing a person's orientation would be equivalent to changing the perceptual world they inhabit motivationally. Does this framing suggest that interventions that only change environmental features (without addressing the person's orientation) are fundamentally limited? What are the implications for intervention design?
> 2. How does the regulatory priming function of orientations interact with explicit goal-setting? If a control-oriented person sets an intrinsic goal (e.g., "I want to learn this for genuine interest"), does the autonomous nature of the goal override the control orientation's priming toward introjected regulation?
> 3. The attentional selectivity mechanism implies that orientations shape what persons literally learn from their experiences over time. What does this suggest about the cumulative, self-reinforcing nature of orientations? Could orientations become progressively more entrenched over development through this mechanism?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. Given that control orientation predicts contingent self-esteem and psychological brittleness, how should practitioners interpret high academic performance from control-oriented students? Is this a form of success worth celebrating, or does it mask a developmental vulnerability?
> 2. The finding that autonomy orientation predicts better long-term health behavior maintenance has practical implications for health behavior change interventions. How should these implications be translated into design principles for public health programs?
> 3. What are the limitations of the correlational evidence base for COT's functional consequence claims? What kinds of experimental or longitudinal designs would provide stronger causal evidence for the mechanisms COT proposes?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. The claim that control-oriented students may find autonomy-supportive environments initially disorienting rather than motivating has significant implications for educational reform. If schools implement autonomy-supportive practices at scale in populations of heavily control-oriented students, what transition challenges might arise, and how should these be managed?
> 2. Motivational Interviewing's effectiveness is well-established empirically. Does understanding MI through the COT lens — as an orientation development intervention — generate novel predictions about when MI will be most or least effective?
> 3. What organizational factors might systematically select for control orientation in career paths? What would an organization deliberately designed to nourish and maintain autonomy orientation in its workforce look like?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. How should COT practitioners respond to the cultural boundedness critique? Is the appropriate response to develop culturally contextualized versions of the theory, or to more radically interrogate the Western assumptions embedded in the original formulation?
> 2. The modest effect sizes of COT predictors (r ≈ .20–.40) are typical for personality and motivational constructs. How should users of the COT framework calibrate their confidence in its practical applications given these effect sizes?
> 3. If longitudinal research demonstrated that orientation is highly malleable throughout adulthood, would this strengthen or weaken the value of COT for educational and clinical practice? What would it mean for the theory's foundational premises?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> 1. When you examine the goals currently embedded in your PKM practice — whether explicit or implicit — do they tend toward mastery orientations (understanding for its own sake) or performance orientations (demonstrating productivity)?
> 2. What specific design features of your current PKB might be inadvertently promoting one orientation over the other?
> 3. How might the concept of process-specific goals (rather than output-specific goals) change the way you structure your daily knowledge work?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> 1. Where on the internalisation continuum (external → introjected → identified → integrated → intrinsic) does your motivation for maintaining your PKB currently reside? What evidence supports your self-assessment?
> 2. If you use gamification features (streaks, counters, badges), have you noticed any changes in how you experience the underlying knowledge work — does it feel more or less inherently interesting than before you introduced these features?
> 3. How might a PKB be designed to systematically support the need for relatedness — often the most neglected need in solitary knowledge management practices — without introducing the competitive comparison dynamics that SDT predicts will undermine autonomous motivation?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> 1. Which phase of the SRL cycle is best scaffolded by your current PKB, and which phase is most neglected?
> 2. Do your review/reflection protocols encourage strategic attributions ("my approach to this topic was insufficiently structured") or ability-based attributions ("I'm just not good at this domain")?
> 3. In Pintrich's terms, how effectively do you regulate your *motivation* and *affect* during knowledge work — and does your PKB provide any infrastructure for this regulation?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> 1. How accurately calibrated are your metacognitive judgements about your own knowledge? Have you ever discovered, upon review, that your understanding of a topic was significantly less (or more) robust than you believed?
> 2. Does your PKB currently include any mechanisms for testing your understanding (retrieval practice, self-questioning, comparison against sources), or does it function primarily as a storage system?
> 3. How might you design a review protocol that progressively fades metacognitive scaffolding as your expertise in a domain develops?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> 1. At which temporal scale is your current PKM assessment practice strongest? Where is it weakest or absent?
> 2. Do your review protocols generate *informational* feedback (here's what you've learned and where you could go deeper) or *evaluative* feedback (here's how you measure up)? What is the motivational consequence?
> 3. How might the concept of self-regulation-level feedback change what questions you ask during your weekly review?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> 1. Which of the four design principles is most strongly instantiated in your current PKB? Which is most absent?
> 2. If you were to implement the five-layer goal scaffolding architecture, what would need to change about your current system — and what transition costs would that entail?
> 3. How might the distinction between "informational" and "controlling" structure change your relationship to the templates and protocols in your PKB?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> 1. At which developmental stage — other-regulated, co-regulated, or self-regulated — do you currently operate in your primary PKM domains? Does the stage differ across domains?
> 2. Does your PKB provide differentiated support for domains where you are a novice versus domains where you are expert?
> 3. What would it mean for your PKB to be designed as a *lifelong* companion — a system that evolves with you across decades rather than being periodically abandoned and rebuilt?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> Before proceeding, consider: what is your current working model of what motivates people to learn, persist, and engage? Do you tend to think of motivation as fundamentally about rewards and punishments, about beliefs and expectations, or about something more deeply organismic — an inherent directionality in human development? SDT takes a strong position on this question, and identifying your prior commitments will help you track where the theory challenges, confirms, or extends your existing understanding.

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> Consider your own most productive learning experiences. Were they characterized merely by the absence of pressure and coercion, or did they involve something actively supportive — a mentor who offered meaningful choices, an environment that provided just-right challenge, a community where you felt genuinely valued? SDT predicts that it is the active presence of need support, not merely the absence of need thwarting, that catalyzes optimal motivation.

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. SDT claims that autonomy means volition, not independence. Can you identify a situation in which you freely chose to submit to authority or follow strict rules? If so, SDT would classify that as autonomous behavior. Does that match your intuitive sense of the experience?
> 2. The internalization continuum proposes that extrinsic motivation can be progressively transformed into self-endorsed regulation. Think of a practice you initially performed out of obligation but now value intrinsically — what happened during the transition? Can you trace it along the continuum?
> 3. Which of the three basic needs — autonomy, competence, or relatedness — do you consider most important for your own learning? SDT predicts that all three are essential. Does the theory's universality claim match your experience?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> Among the motivational frameworks surveyed — SDT, Achievement Goal Theory, Self-Efficacy Theory, Expectancy-Value Theory — which do you find most intuitively compelling? And in what ways does your intuitive preference reflect your own motivational history? SDT would predict that people who have experienced high autonomy support will find organismic explanations more resonant, while those in highly controlling environments may find reinforcement-based explanations more familiar.

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. SDT emerged from the intersection of humanistic psychology and experimental motivation research. What are the strengths and risks of combining phenomenological insight with empirical rigor? Does the combination produce a framework that is stronger than either tradition alone, or does it inherit weaknesses from both?
> 2. The six mini-theories address different phenomena but share a common foundation in basic psychological needs. Does this architectural unity represent theoretical elegance or an overextension of a single explanatory mechanism?
> 3. How would you characterize the relationship between SDT and the other motivational frameworks — complementary, competing, or operating at different levels of analysis?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> Consider the goals you are currently pursuing. Which fall on the intrinsic side (growth, relationships, contribution) and which on the extrinsic side (wealth, status, appearance)? GCT makes a strong empirical prediction: the relative weight of intrinsic versus extrinsic goals in your goal portfolio will predict your well-being independently of how successful you are in achieving them. Does this prediction match your experience?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. SDT claims that all motivational effects operate through a single mediating pathway: basic need satisfaction. Can you think of a motivational phenomenon that this mechanism seems unable to explain? What would it take to falsify the need satisfaction mechanism?
> 2. The internalization process requires autonomy support, competence structure, and relational warmth. In your educational experience, which of these conditions has been most frequently absent? What were the motivational consequences?
> 3. CET predicts that the same external event (e.g., a reward or a piece of feedback) can enhance or undermine intrinsic motivation depending on its functional significance. Can you reconstruct a specific episode where you experienced the same type of event (e.g., praise) as either informational or controlling — and felt the motivational difference?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. SDT-informed teaching emphasizes choice, rationale, and perspective-taking. In your experience, what are the practical barriers to implementing these practices in institutional educational settings? Are there contexts where controlling practices might be defensible?
> 2. The universality debate raises a fundamental question: can a motivational theory developed primarily within Western academic contexts claim universal applicability? What evidence would you need to see to be persuaded either way?
> 3. SDT claims that the pursuit and attainment of extrinsic goals does not produce lasting well-being. Does this claim apply to financial security — or does GCT's prediction hold only above a threshold of material sufficiency?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. What is the single most important insight you have gained from this report? How does it change or enrich your prior understanding of motivation?
> 2. If you were to explain the essence of Self-Determination Theory to a colleague in three sentences, what would you say?
> 3. What is the next question you want to pursue? What has this report made you curious about?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. If the quantitative model of motivation is so inadequate, why does it remain so dominant in educational policy and workplace management? What institutional and cognitive forces sustain it?
> 2. Deci's interpretation of his 1971 findings was theoretically ambitious — he moved from a behavioral observation to a claim about subjective experience of causation. What are the epistemological implications of building a major motivational theory on this kind of interpretive move?
> 3. The organismic-dialectical perspective implies that the question "is this person motivated?" is always incomplete — it must be followed by "and in what kind of environment?" What does this mean for how we design contexts for learning and work?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. SDT's universality claim for the three basic needs is among its most contested aspects. What would constitute decisive evidence that a given need is truly universal versus culturally conditioned? How would you design a study to distinguish these possibilities?
> 2. The claim that autonomy need is compatible with collectivism is theoretically important but empirically complex. What aspects of Eastern educational or family traditions might support autonomy need satisfaction, and what aspects might frustrate it? Does the answer depend on whether students/children endorse the values their culture transmits?
> 3. The relatedness-autonomy relationship described in the Claude insight callout above implies that certain forms of love and care are actually undermining. What are the practical implications of this for parenting, teaching, and therapeutic relationships?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. The introjection trap suggests that people can believe themselves to be autonomously motivated when they are actually operating under internal coercion. What signals in one's own psychology might distinguish genuine autonomous motivation from introjected regulation? What would you look for?
> 2. SDT predicts that regulations can move toward greater autonomy through internalization under the right conditions. But the theory also documents cases where internalization fails or reverses. What conditions might cause a person who was previously intrinsically motivated to shift toward controlled regulation or amotivation?
> 3. Integrated regulation is described as the most complete form of extrinsic motivation and as sharing qualities with intrinsic motivation. Is this distinction theoretically necessary? Could all genuinely valued activity simply be described as intrinsic once it is integrated?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. SDT research shows that even positive controlling feedback ("good job") can undermine intrinsic motivation. How would you explain this to a manager or teacher who views expressing approval as a form of caring? What makes the difference between care that supports autonomy and care that undermines it?
> 2. The warning callout distinguishes autonomy support from permissiveness. Can you identify educational or organizational contexts where this conflation is especially likely to cause problems? What structural changes would help?
> 3. The functional significance framework implies that the same feedback statement could be informational for one person and controlling for another, depending on the relationship context. What are the implications of this for designing feedback systems at scale (such as automated feedback in educational technology)?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. The six mini-theories are presented as addressing different aspects of one integrated framework. Is this integration convincing, or do you see tensions between the different mini-theories that suggest they might be difficult to reconcile? Where are the points of potential conflict?
> 2. Goal Contents Theory's claim that extrinsic goal pursuit undermines well-being even upon attainment challenges powerful cultural norms. What are the social and institutional forces that promote extrinsic goal orientation, and how does SDT's framework help explain why these forces are difficult to resist?
> 3. Causality Orientations Theory proposes that dispositional orientations emerge from developmental need satisfaction history. What are the implications of this for educational systems that encounter students with strongly controlled or impersonal orientations — is it realistic to shift these orientations through educational intervention alone?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. The cross-cultural evidence supports need universality while documenting variation in how needs are expressed and satisfied. How strong is the distinction between universal needs and culturally variable expressions? At what point does variation in expression become so great that it challenges the universality claim itself?
> 2. What does the replication crisis evidence teach us about interpreting psychological research more generally? What methodological standards should a sophisticated consumer of psychological theory maintain?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. Which of these criticisms do you find most compelling, and why? Which does SDT handle most satisfactorily in your judgment?
> 2. If the integration-introjection distinction is primarily functional rather than phenomenological, what research paradigms would be most useful for testing it? What longitudinal outcome variables would most cleanly differentiate integrated from introjected regulation?
> 3. How should practitioners navigate the autonomy-structure tension in their own domains? What heuristics distinguish genuinely autonomy-supportive structure from covertly controlling structure?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. Across these domains, a consistent pattern emerges: autonomous motivation predicts *quality* of outcomes more reliably than *quantity*. What are the implications of this for how we design incentive systems, accountability structures, and performance metrics in each domain?
> 2. SDT's healthcare applications face the practical challenge that healthcare systems are often designed around compliance models (patients following medical advice), which are antithetical to autonomous motivation models. What structural changes would be necessary to enable SDT-based healthcare at scale?

> [!reflection] **Reflect** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> 1. SDT's critique of compliance-based institutional design is sweeping. What legitimate functions do compliance-based systems serve that fully need-supportive designs might struggle to replicate?
> 2. The claim that SDT has philosophical implications for how institutions should be designed represents a move from descriptive science to normative prescription. Under what conditions is this move legitimate? What additional arguments beyond the empirical evidence are needed to make the normative case?
> 3. How does SDT's eudaimonic vision of human flourishing compare to other contemporary accounts (positive psychology's PERMA model, Nussbaum's capabilities approach, Sen's human development framework)? What does SDT add or omit relative to these alternatives?


























































































## Spaced Repetition Seeds

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Definition: What are the three causality orientations in COT?
> **A:** **Q:** Name and briefly define the three causality orientations identified by Deci and Ryan (1985).
> **A:** (1) **Autonomy orientation** — tendency to perceive informational, choice-affording environmental features and regulate behavior through self-endorsement; (2) **Control orientation** — tendency to perceive evaluative, contingent-reward features and regulate behavior through introjection and external pressure; (3) **Impersonal orientation** — tendency to perceive ability-exceeding, causally disconnected features and experience amotivation.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Definition: What is the GCOS?
> **A:** **Q:** What is the General Causality Orientations Scale (GCOS) and how does it measure orientations?
> **A:** The GCOS is a self-report questionnaire presenting 12 vignettes with motivationally relevant situations. For each vignette, respondents rate three response options (autonomy, control, impersonal) on Likert scales. The three orientations are scored as continuous, independent dimensions — not mutually exclusive categories.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Distinction: Autonomy orientation vs. Independence
> **A:** **Q:** How does autonomy orientation in COT differ from independence or self-sufficiency?
> **A:** Autonomy orientation refers to volitional self-endorsement of behavior — acting from genuine willingness and value identification. It is NOT independence from others. An autonomy-oriented person can be deeply connected to and influenced by others while still experiencing their behavior as self-determined. The key is endorsement, not isolation.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Distinction: Contingent vs. Non-contingent self-esteem
> **A:** **Q:** How do control orientation and autonomy orientation relate to different forms of self-esteem?
> **A:** Control orientation produces **contingent self-esteem** — self-regard that fluctuates with performance, approval, and comparative standing. Autonomy orientation supports **non-contingent (genuine) self-esteem** — stable self-regard grounded in the organismic self, not dependent on meeting external standards. The same objective level of competence produces different self-esteem quality depending on orientation.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Process: The motivational affordance mechanism
> **A:** **Q:** How do causality orientations shape motivational responses to environments through the affordance mechanism?
> **A:** Orientations create different perceptual fields for the same objective environment. Autonomy-oriented persons perceive informational, interest-relevant features as salient. Control-oriented persons perceive evaluative, contingent features. Impersonally-oriented persons perceive ability-exceeding features. The same environment affords different motivational responses because the perceptual processing architecture differs.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Process: Developmental pathways to the three orientations
> **A:** **Q:** What developmental conditions produce each of the three causality orientations?
> **A:** **Autonomy orientation** develops from consistent autonomy-supportive parenting (choice, rationale, perspective-taking, informational feedback). **Control orientation** develops from conditional regard, contingent approval, and evaluative pressure. **Impersonal orientation** develops from chaotic, unpredictable, or consistently unresponsive environments where actions fail to produce reliable outcomes.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Application: COT in health behavior change
> **A:** **Q:** Why does autonomy orientation predict better long-term health behavior maintenance?
> **A:** Long-term maintenance requires autonomous regulation — the person must genuinely value the behavior and make it their own. Autonomy-oriented persons are dispositionally better positioned for this internalization. Control-oriented persons can achieve short-term change through compliance but depend on ongoing external motivational infrastructure that is difficult to sustain indefinitely.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Application: COT as moderator in education
> **A:** **Q:** How does COT moderate the effects of autonomy-supportive teaching?
> **A:** Autonomy-oriented students show particularly large benefits from autonomy-supportive environments (the affordances match their perceptual strengths). Control-oriented students may initially find autonomy support disorienting or anxiety-provoking because it conflicts with their expectation of evaluative structure. Effective implementation requires scaffolded transitions calibrated to student orientation profiles.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Connection: COT × OIT regulatory mapping
> **A:** **Q:** How do the three orientations map onto Organismic Integration Theory's regulatory continuum?
> **A:** Autonomy orientation → integrated and identified regulation (self-endorsed, value-based). Control orientation → introjected and external regulation (pressure-based, contingent). Impersonal orientation → amotivation (disconnected from regulatory processes entirely). Orientations function as regulatory primers that set differential thresholds for different regulatory forms.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Distinction: COT vs. Locus of Control
> **A:** **Q:** How does COT differ from Rotter's Locus of Control?
> **A:** Locus of Control (Rotter) is a unidimensional construct (internal–external) addressing perceived contingency between action and outcome. COT is a three-dimensional construct addressing the qualitative character of how people orient toward their motivational environment — not just where control is perceived but what kind of regulatory relationship the person establishes with the environment. COT encompasses the perceived-competence dimension (impersonal orientation) that LoC does not address.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Connection: COT within the SDT macrotheory
> **A:** **Q:** What unique role does COT play within the six mini-theories of Self-Determination Theory?
> **A:** COT is the individual-difference mini-theory. While CET, OIT, BPNT address situational dynamics, COT explains why the same situations produce different motivational outcomes in different persons. It bridges the gap between SDT's situational predictions and the reality of stable interindividual variation in motivational functioning.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Critical Analysis: Cultural boundary conditions
> **A:** **Q:** What is the main cross-cultural critique of COT?
> **A:** COT's autonomy construct may reflect Western assumptions about individual self-determination. In collectivist cultures, the distinction between autonomous and controlled regulation may not map cleanly onto motivational experience — what appears as controlled regulation by Western coding may carry genuine autonomous significance for relationally embedded selves. SDT proponents argue the need for autonomy is universal but its expression is culturally variable — a claim the evidence complicates but does not decisively refute.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 1 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What are the four mechanisms through which goals affect performance according to Locke and Latham's Goal-Setting Theory?
> **A:** Goals (1) direct attention towards goal-relevant activities, (2) energise effort proportional to goal difficulty, (3) promote persistence, and (4) stimulate the discovery and use of task-relevant strategies.
> **Source:** Section 1 | **Difficulty:** Basic | **Tags:** #goal-setting-theory #locke-latham

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 2 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is the key distinction between mastery-approach and performance-approach goals in Elliot's 2×2 Achievement Goal framework?
> **A:** Mastery-approach goals are oriented toward developing competence (learning, improving); performance-approach goals are oriented toward demonstrating competence relative to others (outperforming, proving ability). Both have approach valence, but mastery goals are associated with deeper processing and intrinsic interest while performance goals are associated with surface strategies.
> **Source:** Section 1 | **Difficulty:** Intermediate | **Tags:** #achievement-goal-theory #elliot

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 3 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three basic psychological needs identified by Self-Determination Theory, and why do they matter for PKM?
> **A:** Autonomy (experiencing choice and volitional engagement), competence (feeling effective and capable), and relatedness (feeling connected to others). In PKM, satisfaction of these needs is associated with autonomous motivation — which predicts deeper learning, greater persistence, more creative processing, and higher well-being than controlled motivation.
> **Source:** Section 2 | **Difficulty:** Basic | **Tags:** #sdt #basic-psychological-needs

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 4 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three phases of Zimmerman's cyclical model of self-regulated learning?
> **A:** (1) Forethought — goal-setting, strategic planning, self-efficacy beliefs; (2) Performance — self-monitoring, strategy implementation, attention focusing; (3) Self-reflection — self-evaluation, causal attribution, adaptive revision. These phases cycle continually rather than following a one-time linear sequence.
> **Source:** Section 3 | **Difficulty:** Basic | **Tags:** #srl #zimmerman

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 5 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does the GRM Triad differ from a linear model of goal → regulation → metacognition?
> **A:** The GRM Triad treats goal formulation, self-regulation, and metacognitive monitoring as three simultaneously active vertices of a dynamic system, each capable of initiating change in the others, rather than as sequential stages. This means metacognitive monitoring can revise goals directly (not just evaluate performance), and goal changes can alter the character of monitoring (not just provide targets).
> **Source:** Section 4 | **Difficulty:** Advanced | **Tags:** #grm-triad #original-contribution

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 6 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is the difference between informational and controlling assessment, and why does it matter for PKM?
> **A:** Informational assessment provides data about learning state (here's what you know, here's where gaps remain) and supports autonomy. Controlling assessment evaluates the person's worth (you're doing well/poorly) and creates pressure to perform. Informational assessment sustains intrinsic motivation; controlling assessment undermines it. PKB review protocols should generate informational, not controlling, feedback.
> **Source:** Section 5 | **Difficulty:** Intermediate | **Tags:** #assessment #sdt #feedback

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 7 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** Name the five layers of the PKB Goal Scaffolding Architecture and the temporal scale at which each operates.
> **A:** (1) Direction Layer — long-term, quarterly review; (2) Project Layer — bounded periods, monthly review; (3) Cycle Layer — fortnightly, implements forethought; (4) Session Layer — single sessions, implements performance + reflection; (5) Assessment Layer — multi-scale (micro/meso/macro), provides informational feedback.
> **Source:** Section 6 | **Difficulty:** Intermediate | **Tags:** #pkb-architecture #original-contribution

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 8 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three developmental stages of self-regulatory capacity in PKM, and what characterises each?
> **A:** (1) Other-regulated — relies on external scaffolding (templates, prescribed protocols, community guidance), goals often adopted from external sources. (2) Co-regulated — internalising regulatory processes while benefiting from external support; increasing self-generation of goals. (3) Self-regulated — internalised GRM Triad processes operating fluently; PKB as extended mind rather than external support. Even self-regulated learners revert to other-regulation in new domains.
> **Source:** Section 7 | **Difficulty:** Intermediate | **Tags:** #developmental-trajectory #self-regulation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 9 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is metacognitive calibration, and why is it a central concern for PKM practice?
> **A:** Metacognitive calibration is the accuracy of monitoring judgements relative to actual performance — the degree to which what you think you know matches what you actually know. Poor calibration (overconfidence or underconfidence) leads to misallocated study effort, premature closure on partially understood topics, or unnecessary review of mastered material. Calibration is learnable and improves with domain experience and deliberate self-testing.
> **Source:** Section 4 | **Difficulty:** Advanced | **Tags:** #metacognition #calibration

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three basic psychological needs in Self-Determination Theory?
> **A:** Autonomy (volition and self-endorsement), Competence (effectance and mastery), and Relatedness (meaningful connection with others).
> **Source:** Phase II — Conceptual Foundations
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Type:** Definition
> **Tags:** SDT, basic-needs, motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the critical distinction between autonomous motivation and controlled motivation in SDT?
> **A:** Autonomous motivation involves a sense of volition and self-endorsement (intrinsic motivation + identified + integrated regulation). Controlled motivation involves acting under pressure from external contingencies or internal pressures like guilt (external + introjected regulation). This distinction is the strongest predictor of learning quality and well-being.
> **Source:** Phase II — The Autonomous–Controlled Motivation Distinction
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Type:** Distinction
> **Tags:** SDT, autonomous-motivation, controlled-motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What does "autonomy" mean in SDT — and what does it NOT mean?
> **A:** Autonomy means volition — the experience of self-endorsement in one's actions. It does NOT mean independence, self-reliance, or freedom from influence. A person can autonomously choose to follow rules, cooperate, or defer to authority if they endorse those actions as consistent with their values.
> **Source:** Phase II — Core Concept: Autonomy
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Type:** Distinction
> **Tags:** SDT, autonomy, common-misconceptions

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** Name the six mini-theories that comprise Self-Determination Theory.
> **A:** (1) Cognitive Evaluation Theory (CET), (2) Organismic Integration Theory (OIT), (3) Basic Psychological Needs Theory (BPNT), (4) Causality Orientations Theory (COT), (5) Goal Contents Theory (GCT), (6) Relationship Motivation Theory (RMT).
> **Source:** Phase III — The Six Mini-Theories
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Type:** Definition
> **Tags:** SDT, mini-theories, structure

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the "undermining effect" and what does Cognitive Evaluation Theory predict about it?
> **A:** The undermining effect occurs when tangible external rewards offered contingently for engaging in an interesting task decrease intrinsic motivation. CET explains this through the concept of functional significance: when rewards are experienced as controlling (shifting the perceived locus of causality externally), intrinsic motivation decreases.
> **Source:** Phase III — CET; Phase IV — CET Mediating Appraisals
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Type:** Process
> **Tags:** SDT, CET, undermining-effect, rewards

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the internalization continuum in Organismic Integration Theory?
> **A:** A continuum of regulatory styles from external regulation (behavior controlled by external contingencies) through introjected regulation (internal pressure/guilt) through identified regulation (conscious valuing) through integrated regulation (full assimilation into self-system) to intrinsic motivation (inherent interest). The continuum represents progressively greater internalization.
> **Source:** Phase II — The Autonomous–Controlled Motivation Distinction
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Type:** Process
> **Tags:** SDT, OIT, internalization, regulation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the difference between need satisfaction, need deprivation, and need frustration in SDT?
> **A:** Need satisfaction = active fulfillment of needs (experiencing volition, effectance, connection). Need deprivation = absence of need satisfaction without active thwarting. Need frustration = active thwarting of needs (experiencing coercion, incompetence, rejection). Frustration uniquely predicts ill-being beyond what low satisfaction accounts for.
> **Source:** Phase II — Need Satisfaction and Need Frustration
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Type:** Distinction
> **Tags:** SDT, BPNT, need-frustration, need-satisfaction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is "functional significance" in Cognitive Evaluation Theory?
> **A:** The perceived meaning of an external event with respect to its controlling versus informational aspects. The same event (e.g., praise) can be experienced as primarily controlling (pressuring behavior) or primarily informational (enhancing perceived competence), depending on delivery style and context. Functional significance determines the event's effect on intrinsic motivation.
> **Source:** Phase IV — Cognitive Evaluation Theory
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Type:** Definition
> **Tags:** SDT, CET, functional-significance

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What three contextual conditions does OIT identify as supporting internalization?
> **A:** (1) Autonomy support — choice, rationale, perspective-acknowledgment. (2) Competence structure — scaffolding, informational feedback, optimal challenge. (3) Relational warmth — genuine care, trust, involvement. All three facilitate the movement from external regulation toward identified and integrated regulation.
> **Source:** Phase IV — The Internalization Process
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Type:** Process
> **Tags:** SDT, OIT, internalization, autonomy-support

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** How does Goal Contents Theory distinguish between intrinsic and extrinsic aspirations?
> **A:** Intrinsic aspirations (personal growth, community contribution, relationships, health) are directly linked to need satisfaction. Extrinsic aspirations (wealth, fame, appearance, power) function as need substitutes that fail to deliver well-being even when attained. The relative emphasis on intrinsic vs. extrinsic goals predicts well-being independently of success.
> **Source:** Phase IV — The Goal Content Mechanism
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Type:** Distinction
> **Tags:** SDT, GCT, aspirations, goal-contents

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is autonomy support in SDT, and how does it differ from permissiveness?
> **A:** Autonomy support is a set of interpersonal practices: offering meaningful choice, providing rationales, acknowledging perspectives, minimizing controlling language, and supporting initiative. It is NOT permissiveness or laissez-faire teaching — autonomy support is fully compatible with clear structure and expectations, provided they are delivered non-controllingly.
> **Source:** Phase V — Autonomy-Supportive Teaching
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Type:** Distinction
> **Tags:** SDT, autonomy-support, teaching, misconceptions

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three causality orientations in SDT's COT?
> **A:** (1) Autonomy orientation — tendency to seek and respond to autonomy cues, regulate autonomously. (2) Control orientation — tendency to attend to rewards, demands, and regulate through external/introjected pressure. (3) Impersonal orientation — tendency toward amotivation, helplessness, and perceived incompetence. These are dimensional, not categorical.
> **Source:** Phase IV — Causality Orientations
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Type:** Definition
> **Tags:** SDT, COT, individual-differences

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** How does SDT relate to Self-Regulated Learning (SRL)?
> **A:** SDT and SRL are complementary, not competing. SDT explains *why* learners self-regulate (motivational energetics — autonomous motivation drives deeper SRL engagement). SRL models explain *how* they self-regulate (cognitive-metacognitive processes). Autonomous motivation predicts more effective self-regulatory behavior than controlled motivation.
> **Source:** Phase V — Implications for Self-Regulation
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Type:** Connection
> **Tags:** SDT, SRL, integration, metacognition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the main criticism of SDT's universality claim?
> **A:** Critics argue that the emphasis on autonomy privileges Western individualistic values and may not apply to collectivist cultures. SDT responds that autonomy means volition (not independence) and that volition is valued across cultures. Cross-cultural evidence largely supports universality, though behavioral expressions of need satisfaction vary. The WEIRD sample bias remains a limitation.
> **Source:** Phase V — Limitations
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Type:** Application
> **Tags:** SDT, universality, cross-cultural, criticism

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the organismic metatheory underlying SDT?
> **A:** The philosophical position that human beings are inherently active organisms with natural tendencies toward psychological growth, integration, and elaboration of a unified self. Unlike mechanistic models, the organismic perspective treats humans as proactive agents whose development depends on environmental satisfaction of basic psychological needs.
> **Source:** Phase II — The Organismic Metatheoretical Commitment
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Type:** Definition
> **Tags:** SDT, metatheory, organismic, philosophy

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Definition: Basic Psychological Need
> **A:** **Q**: How does SDT define a "basic psychological need," and how does this differ from ordinary use of the word "need"?
> **A**: In SDT, a basic psychological need is a *nutriment* whose satisfaction is essential for ongoing psychological growth, integrity, and well-being, and whose frustration produces psychological ill-health. Unlike ordinary "needs" (desires, preferences, culturally constructed wants), true basic needs are universal, have a distinct satisfaction vs. frustration structure, and have proven predictive and mediational roles across diverse cultures and contexts.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Definition: Organismic-Dialectical Meta-Theory
> **A:** **Q**: What is the organismic-dialectical perspective, and why does it matter for SDT?
> **A**: The organismic-dialectical perspective holds that humans are naturally active agents who inherently tend toward growth, integration, and vitality (organismic), but this tendency must be continually negotiated with social and environmental conditions that either support or contradict it (dialectical). It grounds SDT's claim that intrinsic motivation and need-satisfaction tendencies are the default — not the exception — and that motivational deficits require explanation through environmental conditions, not simply through human "nature."

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Distinction: Autonomous vs. Controlled Motivation
> **A:** **Q**: Distinguish autonomous from controlled motivation, and give an example of each in an educational context.
> **A**: **Autonomous motivation** = behavior performed because of genuine volition, endorsement, or inherent interest (internal perceived locus of causality). *Example*: A student reads extra material because they find the topic fascinating. **Controlled motivation** = behavior performed because of external pressure, reward contingency, or introjected pride/shame pressure. *Example*: A student reads extra material because they fear a teacher's disapproval if they don't. Same behavior, different quality — different outcomes for deep learning and persistence.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Distinction: Introjection vs. Identification
> **A:** **Q**: What is the difference between introjected and identified regulation? What are the different phenomenological and functional consequences?
> **A**: **Introjected regulation**: The rule or value has been taken in but is maintained through ego-involvement — internal pressure, shame if violated, pride if met. Feels like "I should" or "I have to." Fragile under challenge (shame-based collapse). **Identified regulation**: The value has been consciously endorsed as personally important — maintained by genuine belief in its importance. Feels like "I want to because it matters to me." More resilient, leads to deeper engagement.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Process: The Functional Significance Model (CET)
> **A:** **Q**: What is "functional significance" in Cognitive Evaluation Theory, and how does it determine whether an extrinsic event enhances or undermines intrinsic motivation?
> **A**: Functional significance is the perceived meaning of an extrinsic event for the individual's autonomy and competence. Three functional significances: (1) **Informational** — provides competence-relevant data while respecting autonomy → supports or enhances intrinsic motivation. (2) **Controlling** — pressures toward specified behavior → shifts PLOC external and undermines intrinsic motivation. (3) **Amotivating** — communicates incompetence with no information for improvement → reduces both intrinsic motivation and competence need satisfaction.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Application: Designing Autonomy-Supportive Feedback
> **A:** **Q**: What distinguishes informational from controlling feedback? Give a concrete example of each for the same performance.
> **A**: **Informational**: Specifies what was done effectively, what would improve the work, and why — referencing the person's goals and growth, not an evaluative standard. *Example*: "Your analysis in paragraphs 2-3 is clear and well-supported. Adding a counterargument checkpoint would strengthen your claim — does that fit what you're trying to argue?" **Controlling**: Evaluates performance relative to a standard, often with implicit approval/disapproval. *Example*: "This isn't quite at the level I expect. You need to do better." Same factual gap in quality; very different motivational and emotional consequences.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Definition: Need Frustration vs. Need Non-Satisfaction
> **A:** **Q**: Why does SDT distinguish between *need frustration* and simple *absence of need satisfaction*? What does the distinction predict?
> **A**: Need frustration involves the *active thwarting* of a need — being forced to comply despite one's own values (autonomy frustration), being told one is incompetent or facing repeated failures without support (competence frustration), or experiencing conditional regard or exclusion (relatedness frustration). Simple *absence* of satisfaction is motivationally neutral. SDT/BPNT predicts that need frustration is the primary predictor of psychological ill-health (depression, anxiety, burnout), while mere absence of satisfaction produces motivational neutrality. Removing controlling conditions doesn't produce flourishing — active need support is required.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Connection: SDT and Eudaimonic Well-Being
> **A:** **Q**: How does SDT's account of well-being relate to the classical philosophical concept of eudaimonia? What does SDT add to the philosophical tradition?
> **A**: Both identify well-being with *full functioning* — vital engagement with meaningful activities and relationships — rather than with pleasure/happiness alone. SDT adds *empirical operationalizability*: need satisfaction scales, RAI measures, and vitality instruments translate the eudaimonic ideal into testable predictions. SDT also provides *causal mechanisms* (need satisfaction → integration → vitality) that philosophical accounts lack. The cost is that SDT's operationalization necessarily narrows and qualifies the broader philosophical concept.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Application: SDT in Healthcare Contexts
> **A:** **Q**: SDT predicts that *autonomous* patient motivation predicts sustained behavior change better than controlled motivation. What does this imply for practitioner communication style in chronic disease management?
> **A**: Practitioners should: (1) Elicit and amplify patients' own reasons for behavior change (not impose the practitioner's reasons); (2) Provide informational rather than directive guidance; (3) Acknowledge and validate any ambivalence rather than pressuring toward compliance; (4) Explain *why* recommendations matter for the patient's own goals, not just the medical protocol; (5) Maintain unconditional positive regard toward the patient irrespective of adherence. These principles — which parallel the [[Motivational-Interviewing]] approach — are predicted by SDT to produce autonomous motivation that persists beyond the clinical encounter.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Definition: Causality Orientation
> **A:** **Q**: What are SDT's three causality orientations, and how do they develop?
> **A**: **Autonomy orientation**: Tendency to perceive environments in terms of their informational value and to seek intrinsic reasons for behavior. Develops from histories of need-supportive environments. **Control orientation**: Tendency to perceive environments in controlling terms and to use external or ego-involving criteria as motivational anchors. Develops from histories of controlling environments. **Impersonal orientation**: Tendency to perceive outcomes as unrelated to own effort — the dispositional correlate of [[Amotivation]]. Develops from histories of unresponsive or chaotic environments. Orientations are stable but malleable with sustained environmental changes.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Distinction: Intrinsic vs. Extrinsic Goal Contents (GCT)
> **A:** **Q**: In Goal Contents Theory, what distinguishes intrinsic from extrinsic goal contents, and what does GCT predict about their relationship to well-being?
> **A**: **Intrinsic goals** (personal growth, meaningful relationships, community contribution, health) directly satisfy basic psychological needs when pursued and attained. **Extrinsic goals** (wealth, status, fame, appearance) do not inherently satisfy needs — their attainment depends on external validation and may leave needs unmet. GCT predicts that orienting one's life strongly around extrinsic goals is associated with lower well-being, higher anxiety, and the *attainment paradox* (achieving wealth/fame goals produces less well-being than anticipated while intrinsic goals produce more).

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> **Q:** Process: Internalization — Conditions
> **A:** **Q**: What conditions does SDT propose facilitate deep internalization (integration) of initially external regulations?
> **A**: Three conditions: (1) **Relational context**: Genuine relatedness with a significant other who transmits the value — people internalize values from people they care about and feel cared for by. (2) **Rationale-giving**: Providing a meaningful explanation of *why* the regulation is important that connects to the person's own values and goals — not just asserting its importance. (3) **Choice and voice**: Allowing some degree of input into the regulation itself, acknowledging difficulties, and minimizing controlling pressure. Without these conditions, regulations may produce compliance (external regulation) or guilt-driven self-imposition (introjection) but not genuine integration.

















































## Key Figures & Intellectual Lineage

> [!person] **Edward L. Deci (1942–2017)** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> Co-creator of Self-Determination Theory and co-developer of Causality Orientations Theory. Deci's early experimental work on the effects of extrinsic rewards on intrinsic motivation (Deci, 1971) provided the empirical foundation for what would become Cognitive Evaluation Theory, and eventually the broader SDT framework. With Ryan, he developed COT's three-orientation taxonomy and the GCOS instrument. Deci's theoretical emphasis was consistently on the organismic tendency toward self-determination as a fundamental feature of human nature.

> [!person] **Richard M. Ryan (b. 1953)** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> Co-creator of Self-Determination Theory and co-developer of COT. Ryan brought clinical psychological perspectives to SDT, contributing particularly to the theory's treatment of internalization, ego-involvement, and the relationship between need satisfaction and psychological well-being. Ryan's influence on COT is visible in the theory's sophisticated treatment of control orientation's relationship to introjected regulation and contingent self-worth.

> [!person] **Wendy S. Grolnick** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> Developmental psychologist who conducted foundational research on the parenting antecedents of causality orientations and autonomous motivation. Grolnick's work on parental autonomy support versus control provided empirical grounding for COT's developmental claims and established the mechanisms through which parenting practices shape orientation development.

> [!person] **Richard Koestner** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> Researcher who contributed significantly to the empirical validation of COT, particularly through studies examining the interaction between causality orientations and situational factors in predicting intrinsic motivation and performance outcomes. Koestner's work demonstrated that orientations moderate the effects of feedback, rewards, and evaluative contexts.

> [!person] **Maarten Vansteenkiste** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> Contemporary SDT researcher who has advanced the understanding of need frustration (versus mere absence of satisfaction) and conducted extensive cross-cultural investigations of SDT constructs. His work has implications for COT's cross-cultural generalizability and for understanding the developmental pathways through which need-thwarting environments produce control and impersonal orientations.

> [!person] **Richard deCharms (1927–2013)** *(from [[causality-orientations-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> Intellectual precursor to SDT. deCharms's distinction between "Origins" (persons who perceive themselves as the origin of their behavior) and "Pawns" (persons who perceive themselves as moved by external forces) prefigured Deci and Ryan's autonomy-control distinction. deCharms's classroom intervention work, demonstrating that students could be helped to develop more Origin-like orientations, established the applied foundation for COT's developmental and intervention implications.

> [!person] **Edwin Locke (b. 1938) & Gary Latham (b. 1944)** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> American organisational psychologists who jointly developed Goal-Setting Theory through a research programme spanning four decades. Their 1990 monograph *A Theory of Goal Setting and Task Performance* and 2002 review constitute the canonical statement of the theory. Locke's contribution centred on the philosophical foundations (goal-directed action as a basic feature of human nature), while Latham contributed the applied and experimental dimensions. Their work is foundational for Section 1 of this report.
> 
> **Key works:** Locke & Latham (1990, 2002, 2006).

> [!person] **Carol Dweck (b. 1946)** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> American developmental psychologist at Stanford University whose research on implicit theories of intelligence and achievement goals introduced the mastery vs. performance goal distinction (initially termed learning goals vs. performance goals). Her later popularisation of "growth mindset" extends this work to broader audiences. Within this report, her contribution provides the mastery orientation principle central to Sections 1, 2, and 6.
> 
> **Key works:** Dweck (1986), Dweck & Leggett (1988), Dweck (2006).
> 
> **Relationship:** Built on Nicholls' (1984) earlier distinction between task-involvement and ego-involvement; extended by Elliot's (1999) 2×2 framework.

> [!person] **Edward Deci (1942–2022) & Richard Ryan (b. 1953)** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> American psychologists who co-developed Self-Determination Theory across four decades of collaborative research at the University of Rochester. Deci's early experimental work on the undermining effects of external rewards on intrinsic motivation (1971) launched the research programme; Ryan contributed the theoretical architecture, particularly the organismic integration framework. Their work provides the motivational foundation for Sections 2, 5, and 6.
> 
> **Key works:** Deci (1971), Deci & Ryan (1985, 2000), Ryan & Deci (2017).

> [!person] **Barry Zimmerman (1942–2020)** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> American educational psychologist at the City University of New York whose cyclical model of self-regulated learning became one of the most influential frameworks in the SRL literature. Zimmerman's contribution was to specify the *phases* and *sub-processes* of SRL with sufficient precision to guide both research and intervention. His work is the primary SRL framework employed in Section 3.
> 
> **Key works:** Zimmerman (1989, 2000, 2002).
> 
> **Relationship:** Complementary to Pintrich's (2000) framework, which emphasises the *areas* of regulation; parallel to Winne and Hadwin's (1998) information-processing approach to SRL.

> [!person] **Thomas Nelson (1943–2005) & Louis Narens** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> American cognitive psychologists who developed the two-level metacognitive monitoring framework that distinguishes object-level cognition from meta-level monitoring and control. Their framework provides the theoretical architecture for the metacognitive analysis in Section 4 and is a vertex of the GRM Triad.
> 
> **Key works:** Nelson & Narens (1990, 1994).

> [!person] **[[Edward-Deci]] (1942–2022)** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> Co-founder of Self-Determination Theory. Professor of Psychology and Gowen Professor in the Social Sciences at the University of Rochester. Deci's groundbreaking 1971 experiments on the undermining effect of external rewards on intrinsic motivation launched the research program that would become SDT. His 1975 book *Intrinsic Motivation* and 1985 collaboration with Ryan (*Intrinsic Motivation and Self-Determination in Human Behavior*) established the foundational framework.
> 
> **Lineage:** Influenced by [[Robert-White]]'s concept of [[Effectance|effectance motivation]] and [[Carl-Rogers]]'s organismic psychology. Co-developed SDT with [[Richard-Ryan]]. Supervised and influenced [[Maarten-Vansteenkiste]], Christopher Niemiec, and numerous other SDT researchers.
> 
> **Relationship to Report:** Primary architect of the theoretical framework described in every section of this report.

> [!person] **[[Richard-Ryan]] (1953–)** *(from [[self-determination-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-01]])*
> Co-founder of Self-Determination Theory. Professor at the Institute for Positive Psychology and Education at the Australian Catholic University and Research Professor at the University of Rochester.
